We had a bright blue one hanging from the oak tree in our front yard for years. The blue jays loved it. Though blue jays can be awfully mean to other birds, can't they? I saw one chasing a cardinal away from the feeder just last week. It's a shame, because the cardinals are so pretty against the snow in the wintertime. \n\nBut... oh my goodness, I'm doing it again. I'm sorry, doctor. What did you ask me about being outside?\n\nDoctor: Are you also afraid of other insects, like bees or spiders?\n\nPatient: Bees or spiders? Oh, no, not at all. Just the wasps. Bees are actually quite nice, aren't they? They make honey and pollinate the flowers. I planted a whole row of lavender along the side of the house just for the bees. \n\nLavender smells so wonderful, especially after a light rain. I used to buy these little lavender sachets from a woman at the farmer's market to put in my dresser drawers. It keeps the moths away from your sweaters, you know. Or at least that's what my grandmother always told me. She had this beautiful cedar chest at the foot of her bed where she kept all her heavy winter quilts. It was hand-carved, with these intricate little flowers all along the edges. I always wanted that chest, but my cousin Martha ended up taking it when Grandma passed. \n\nMartha always did get whatever she wanted. She was the youngest, you see. Spoiled rotten. Even when we were little girls, she'd throw an absolute fit if she didn't get the pink frosting on her cupcake. And my aunt would always just give in to her. It used to make me so mad. I remember one time at my tenth birthday party, she actually blew out my candles before I could! I cried for an hour in my bedroom. \n\nWait... what was I saying? Oh, I'm so sorry. I get so carried away. Were you asking about my cousin Martha?\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of all types of wasps or just certain ones?\n\nPatient: All of them, really. Yellow jackets, hornets, paper wasps... if it looks like a wasp, I just can't be near it. Though those big bald-faced hornets are probably the most terrifying. We had a nest of those in the eaves of our garage a few years back. \n\nMy husband had to call an exterminator to come deal with it. The exterminator was a very nice young man, actually. His name was Kevin, I think. Or maybe Kyle? No, it was Kevin. He was wearing this bright green uniform shirt that matched his truck. I remember thinking it was a very cheerful color for someone whose job is killing bugs. He said he had just gotten married the month before. They went to Hawaii for their honeymoon. Maui, I think he said. \n\nI've always wanted to go to Hawaii, but the flight is just so long. I don't do well sitting in one place for that many hours. My legs get all stiff, and the airplane food is always so salty. The last time we flew anywhere was to see my son in Denver, and they gave us these tiny little bags of pretzels. I mean, honestly, there were maybe five pretzels in the whole bag! And the flight attendant looked so annoyed when I asked for a second one. I understand they have a hard job, being up in the air all day, but a little politeness goes a long way, doesn't it? My mother always used to say, \"Manners cost nothing but buy everything.\" She was very big on manners. She used to make us write thank-you notes for every little thing, even if it was just a pair of socks from Aunt Mildred. \n\nAunt Mildred knitted the most uncomfortable socks. The wool was always so scratchy... \n\nOh dear. I'm talking about scratchy socks now, aren't I? I am so sorry. You asked me about the types of wasps, didn't you?\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps mostly about being stung by them or is it more about seeing them or knowing they are around?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... I know a wasp is just a little insect, really. I mean, most folks would just swat it away or ignore it. But to me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen if one gets anywhere near me. It's just this awful, overwhelming feeling. \n\nMy neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he just swats them with his rolled-up newspaper like it's nothing. He gets the Sunday paper delivered every week. The big one, with all the coupons and the color comics. I used to love reading the Sunday funnies when I was a little girl. My dad would spread them out on the living room floor for me and my brother. Family Circus was always my favorite. The little dotted lines showing where the kids ran all over the neighborhood... \n\nWe used to run all over our neighborhood, too, back before they put in that new subdivision. We'd ride our bikes until the streetlights came on. I had a beautiful Schwinn bicycle. It was cherry red with a white basket on the front. I put plastic daisies on the spokes, and they made this wonderful clicking sound when I rode fast. I rode that bike everywhere until I outgrew it. Then my parents gave it to the little girl down the street. Her name was Susie, I think. She had the curliest red hair I'd ever seen. Like little springs all over her head. \n\nHer mother used to try and tame it with these big velvet bows, but it never really worked. Velvet is such a nice fabric, though, isn't it? So soft. I had a velvet dress for Christmas when I was twelve... \n\nOh, goodness gracious. I'm doing it again, aren't I? I am so, so sorry. You were asking about being stung, weren't you?\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps mostly about the pain of being stung or is it something else?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... I suppose it's the pain, yes. The sting itself. I just can't stand the thought of that sharp, burning feeling. It's not like a bee sting, where they lose their stinger and die. Wasps can just keep stinging you over and over again. It's just so malicious, isn't it? \n\nMy uncle Arthur used to say that wasps were just angry at the world. He was a very wise man, Uncle Arthur. He worked as a clockmaker for over forty years. He had this little shop downtown, right next to the old bakery. Oh, the smells that used to come from that bakery! Every morning, they'd make these fresh cinnamon rolls with that thick white icing dripping down the sides. My mother would let me walk down there on Saturday mornings with a shiny silver quarter to buy one. A quarter went a long way back then. You could get a cinnamon roll and still have a nickel left over for the gumball machine. \n\nI always hoped for the blue gumballs, but I usually got the yellow ones. Yellow is such a bright, happy color, though. My kitchen at home is painted yellow. \"Sunshine Yellow,\" the paint can said. I picked it out myself at the hardware store. The man at the counter mixed it right there in front of me in one of those big shaker machines. It made the loudest racket, like a washing machine with a brick in it...\n\nWait, what? Oh, heavens. I've completely lost the thread again, haven't I? The pain of the sting, yes, that's what terrifies me.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear wasps only when you are outside or in a specific location, or is the fear present even when you are safe indoors and not expecting to see a wasp?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's really everywhere, honestly. Even when I'm sitting right in my own living room, with the windows shut tight and the air conditioning on. I just... I can't stop thinking that one might have snuck in somehow. \n\nYou know, like through the chimney or a tiny crack by the door. We have this beautiful heavy oak front door. My husband bought it at an antique salvage yard about ten years ago. It has this gorgeous stained glass panel in the top, with a pattern of lilies and vines. The light shines through it so prettily in the late afternoon. It casts these little rainbows on the hallway floor. \n\nOur cat, Barnaby—he's a big orange tabby, just the sweetest thing—he loves to sleep right in those little rainbow spots. He'll stretch all the way out on his back with his paws in the air. He used to be a mouser, back when we first got him, but now he's just too lazy. The other day I saw a spider right next to his nose, and he didn't even open his eyes! Just kept right on snoring. It's funny how pets get like that when they get older. They just want to be comfortable. \n\nMy husband is getting a bit like that, too, if I'm being honest. He used to love going out and doing yard work on the weekends, but lately, he just wants to sit in his recliner and watch the Golf Channel. I don't understand golf at all. It just seems like a lot of walking around in funny pants to hit a tiny white ball into a hole. But he says it's relaxing to watch. The announcers always whisper so quietly, like they're in a library or something... \n\nOh, my goodness. I'm talking about golf and the cat now. I am so sorry, doctor. You were asking if I'm scared indoors, too, right? Yes, even inside, I just can't shake the feeling that a wasp is going to get me.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps only about the possibility of being stung, or is it also about other things, like their appearance or the noise they make?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... I suppose it's not just the sting, though that's the worst part. But the second I hear that sharp buzzing sound, like out by the porch or if I see one hovering near my petunias, my heart just jumps right up into my throat. My hands start shaking, and I just feel this overwhelming panic, like I have to get inside right away and shut the door. \n\nI planted those petunias myself, you know. I got them at the garden center over on Route 9. They had this huge sale for Memorial Day. Buy two flats, get one free. I ended up getting a flat of the purple ones and two flats of the pink. Pink has always been my favorite color for the garden. My grandmother used to grow the most beautiful pink peonies along the side of her house. They would bloom every year right around my birthday in early June. We used to cut them and put them in these big crystal vases on the dining room table. \n\nThat table was solid mahogany. My grandfather bought it at an estate sale before the war. It was so heavy, it took four grown men to move it when my grandparents downsized to that little apartment on Elm Street. Elm Street used to have actual elm trees, you know, before the Dutch elm disease took them all. It was so sad. They had to come through with these big chainsaws and cut them all down. The neighborhood looked so bare after that. Just completely different, like someone had come in and stolen the roof right off the street...\n\nOh, dear me. I've done it again, haven't I? I am so sorry. I start talking about flowers and before I know it, I'm cutting down trees. But yes, to answer your question, the buzzing sound and just seeing them makes me panic and want to run inside.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of wasps is also because you feel like you can't control them or make them go away?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose that's part of it, yes. They just dart around so fast, and you never know where they're going to land. I know a wasp is just a little insect and most folks would just swat it away with a rolled-up newspaper or something, but to me... it feels like something terrible is about to happen if one gets anywhere near me. I just freeze up. \n\nMy father used to swat flies with a rolled-up newspaper. He always read the Sunday paper from front to back. He loved the crossword puzzles. He'd sit in his big armchair with a cup of black coffee—he never put sugar or cream in it, said it ruined the taste of the beans. He bought his coffee from a little Italian market down by the river. They roasted the beans right there in the shop. You could smell it from three blocks away. \n\nThat whole neighborhood smelled wonderful, really. There was a bakery next to the coffee shop that made these incredible cannolis. My sister Margaret and I would save our allowance to buy them. Margaret was always better at saving money than I was. She ended up working at a bank for thirty years. She started as a teller and worked her way up to branch manager. She always wore these very sharp, tailored suits. Navy blue or charcoal gray, mostly. She said it made people take her more seriously. I always preferred wearing dresses myself. I have this lovely floral dress I bought for a spring wedding a few years ago. It has little daisies all over it...\n\nOh, for heaven's sake. I'm talking about dresses and cannolis now. I am so sorry. You were asking about not being able to control the wasps. Yes, it's just... to me, they aren't just bugs. It feels like this awful, impending doom if they get close, even though I know it's silly.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of wasps caused you to avoid social gatherings or events where you think wasps might be present?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, it really has. I mean, I used to be so involved with the church gardening group. We’d meet every Tuesday morning to tend to the flower beds around the sanctuary. But I just had to step back from it completely. I can't even host my own garden days anymore, which is... well, it’s just heartbreaking because that was a big part of how I stayed connected with my friends. \n\nThe ladies in the group are just wonderful. Mrs. Higgins, she brings these lemon bars every week that are just to die for. She uses real lemon zest, not that extract stuff you buy in the little bottles at the grocery store. I tried making them once, but my crust got all soggy. Baking is such a science, isn't it? You have to measure everything so perfectly. My grandmother used to bake bread every single Sunday. She never measured a thing, just threw flour and water and yeast into this big wooden bowl she brought over from the old country. It always turned out perfectly. The house would smell like warm yeast and butter all afternoon. \n\nWe used to eat it right out of the oven with homemade strawberry jam. We'd pick the strawberries ourselves at Miller's Farm out on County Road 12. Mr. Miller was this tall, lanky man who always wore denim overalls. He had a tractor that he let all the kids climb on. It was a bright red Massey Ferguson. My brother Tommy loved that tractor. He ended up becoming a mechanic, actually. He works at a dealership down in Florida now. It’s so hot down there, though. I don't know how he stands the humidity. My hair just frizzes up the second I step off the plane in Orlando...\n\nOh, good heavens. I'm talking about tractors and Florida now. I'm so sorry, doctor. My mind just wanders. You were asking about avoiding things. Yes, I don't do the gardening group or host anymore. And even at home, my husband has had to take over all the porch and garden chores that I used to handle myself, because I just can't be out there.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else, besides wasps, that triggers a similar fear or panic response in you?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. It's really just the wasps that do this to me. I mean, I don't like spiders or anything, but they don't make me panic like that. I can just step on a spider or trap it under a cup. I have this special plastic cup I keep under the kitchen sink just for catching bugs. It's an old souvenir cup from a trip my husband and I took to Mount Rushmore. \n\nWe went there for our 25th wedding anniversary. We drove the whole way, took us three days just to get out there. The scenery was beautiful, though. We drove through the Badlands, and it looked like something out of a science fiction movie. All those strange rock formations. We stopped at this little diner in South Dakota that served the most amazing cherry pie. The waitress said the cherries were grown locally. She had the brightest red hair I’d ever seen, like Lucille Ball. I always loved Lucille Ball. *I Love Lucy* was my favorite show growing up. My mother and I would watch it every week. She had this loud, wonderful laugh that would just fill the whole living room...\n\nOh, dear. I'm sorry. I've drifted off again, haven't I? Mount Rushmore and cherry pie. But no, to answer your question, it's just the wasps. Nothing else makes me feel that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have a day where you don't think about wasps at all?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... no, not really. Not unless there's a foot of snow on the ground, anyway. During the spring and summer, I'm always looking out for them, checking the eaves and the porch before I even open the door. \n\nSummer used to be my absolute favorite time of year, though. When our kids were little, we'd pack up the station wagon and drive out to the lake every weekend. We had this big, wood-paneled station wagon... a Ford Country Squire, I think it was called. The seats were made of that sticky vinyl that would just burn the back of your legs if you wore shorts. My daughter Emily hated it. She would always bring a big beach towel just to sit on. Emily is an interior designer now, out in Chicago. She has such a wonderful eye for color. She just repainted her whole downstairs this lovely shade of sage green. Green is such a calming color, don't you think? I tried to paint my kitchen green once, oh, years ago, but it ended up looking like split pea soup. My husband had to repaint the whole thing a soft white the very next weekend. He's so handy like that. He actually built the shelves in our pantry completely from scratch using oak wood he got from a friend at the lumber yard. They're so sturdy, you could probably park a car on them...\n\nOh, dear me. I've done it again, haven't I? Station wagons and pantry shelves. I am so sorry, doctor. My train of thought just completely derailed. But to answer your question... no, I don't really have days where I forget about them entirely. They're always hovering somewhere in the back of my mind.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of wasps stopped you from doing things you would normally enjoy or made it hard for you to be around others?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, it really has. I think I mentioned it earlier, but I had to completely step back from the church gardening group. I used to go every Tuesday, and I even hosted my own garden days here at the house. It was such a big part of how I stayed connected with my friends. The garden was always so lovely in the spring, especially when the tulips came up. I had these beautiful Queen of Night tulips—they're this deep, dark purple, almost black. I ordered the bulbs from a catalog that came in the mail. It was one of those thick, glossy catalogs that smells like fresh paper. My mother used to get those same catalogs. She would sit at the kitchen table with a red pen and circle everything she wanted to plant. She always wanted a weeping willow tree, but our yard was just too small. We lived in this little brick house on Elm Street. The street actually didn't have any elm trees left because of Dutch elm disease, which I always thought was a bit ironic. They replaced them all with maples, which are pretty in the fall, but they drop those little helicopter seeds everywhere. They get into the gutters and make such a mess. My husband has to clean the gutters twice a year, and he always complains about his knees when he does it. He played football in high school, you know, and he injured his meniscus...\n\nOh, good heavens, I'm doing it again. I'm so sorry, doctor. I just start talking and my brain goes right out the window. Tulips and gutters. But yes, to answer your question, it's definitely stopped me from doing things. I don't do the gardening group or host anymore, and I can't even go out and do my own porch and garden chores. My husband has had to take over all of that for me.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps more about the possibility of being stung or about seeing them or knowing they are nearby?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it's both, really. Just knowing they're out there, building those little paper nests under the eaves, it makes my chest tight. But the thought of being stung, oh, that's just terrifying. I've never liked needles or anything sharp. When I was a little girl, I had to get my tonsils taken out, and the nurse came in with this needle that looked like it was a foot long. I screamed so loud my father had to come running down the hall. He was wearing his work clothes, these thick denim overalls he wore at the auto shop. He always smelled like motor oil and peppermint. He used to keep those little red and white peppermint candies in his pockets. He said they helped him quit smoking. He used to smoke two packs a day, Pall Malls, I think. The ones without the filters. My mother hated it. She would make him smoke out on the back porch, even in the dead of winter. We lived in Michigan back then, and the winters were just brutal. The snow would pile up so high against the back door we couldn't even open it sometimes. My brother Tommy and I would build these massive snow forts in the front yard. We'd stay out there for hours until our mittens were completely soaked through and our fingers were numb... \n\nOh, for heaven's sake. I'm doing it again, aren't I? Snow forts and peppermints. I really don't know what's wrong with my brain today, doctor, I just can't seem to stay on track. But yes, to answer your question... it's the thought of the sting that really terrifies me, but just seeing them or knowing they're around is enough to set me off.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps something that you experience all the time, even when you're not in a situation where you might encounter them?\n\nPatient: Well, it's funny you ask that, because... I suppose it's not all the time, not every single minute. If I'm inside, and the windows are shut tight, I can usually relax a bit. Especially if I'm busy with something. I like to knit, you see. I'm working on this afghan right now for my nephew's new baby. They're having a little girl in October. They haven't picked a name yet, but they're thinking about Eleanor. I think Eleanor is such a classic name. My great-aunt was named Eleanor, but everyone called her Ellie. She lived in this big Victorian house that had a wraparound porch and a real turret. It was like something out of a fairy tale. She used to collect those little porcelain thimbles from all over the world. She had hundreds of them displayed in this glass cabinet in her parlor. I remember one was from Holland and it had a tiny windmill painted on it. The blades actually moved if you flicked them with your fingernail. My mother always told me not to touch them, but I just couldn't resist. I was a very curious child. I used to take apart the toaster just to see how the heating coils worked. Of course, I could never put it back together right, and my father would have to fix it...\n\nOh, dear. There I go again. Toasters and thimbles. I am so sorry, doctor. I don't know why I can't just give you a straight answer today. But to go back to what you were saying... I guess I'm not really sure. I mean, I don't think about them when I'm safe inside knitting, but I don't know if I'd say I experience the fear all the time when I'm not around them. I'm just not completely certain."
  }
